1. The Cys thiolate (Cys-S⁻) attacks the sulfur atom of the sulfur donor

3. The persulfide sulfur is then transferred to the acceptor substrate

This matches our sequence almost exactly! This is B. subtilis riboflavin synthase (RibC)!

Riboflavin synthase (RibC) from Bacillus subtilis uses FMN (Flavin Mononucleotide) as a tightly bound cofactor. The FMN acts as a catalyst (it accepts and donates electrons during the cyclization of two molecules of 6,7-dimethyl-8-D-ribityllumazine to form riboflavin and lumazine).

So the answer is C. Flavin mononucleotide (FMN).

The FMN is tightly bound and serves as a catalyst in the reaction cycle (it&#x27;s reduced by one molecule of substrate and then oxidized by another molecule of substrate). It&#x27;s not a &quot;reducing reagent&quot; in the sense that it&#x27;s consumed in the reaction - it&#x27;s regenerated. Now, for this TM (170-184, about 15 residues with 87% hydrophobicity), is it long enough? A standard lipid bilayer is about 30 Å thick. An alpha helix rises about 1.5 Å per residue. So we need about 20 residues to span the bilayer. 15 residues would only span about 22.5 Å, which is not enough.

However, in real proteins, TM helices can be:
- Tilted (reducing the effective length needed)
- Slightly longer than the hydrophobic core suggests
- The hydrophobic boundary might be less than the full bilayer thickness

And some TM prediction methods use a minimum of about 17-19 residues for a TM helix. With my extended estimate of 15-18 residues for this region, it&#x27;s borderline.

If this TM is counted, I have 6. If not, I have 5.

Hmm, but let me reconsider once more. Looking at this from a higher level:

- TM2 (113-132)

Hmm, the topology doesn&#x27;t quite work with this. If TM1 has an extracellular domain before it, and TM2 is right after with only 6 residues in between, the topology would be:
- After signal peptide cleavage: Extracellular N-term
- TM1 goes from extracellular to intracellular
- Short loop (6 residues) on the intracellular side
- TM2 goes from intracellular to extracellular
- Long His-rich domain (extracellular)
- TM3 goes from extracellular to intracellular
- Long domain (could be intracellular or extracellular)
- TM4...
etc.

This actually works as a 6-TM topology for a type I membrane protein (or similar).

Actually, looking at this again, I realize the long domain between TM3 (185-302) could be intracellular or extracellular depending on the number of TMs before it. After TM1, TM2, TM3 (3 TMs), the cell would be on the opposite side from the initial extracellular domain. If we started extracellular:
- TM1: Ex → In (1 pass)
- TM2: In → Ex (2 passes, back to Ex)
- TM3: Ex → In (3 passes)
- TM4: In → Ex (4 passes)
- TM5: Ex → In (5 passes)
- TM6: In → Ex (6 passes)

With even number of TMs, the C-terminus would be on the same side as the N-terminus after the signal peptide was cleaved.

This topology makes sense for a 6-TM protein.

OK, I&#x27;m going with 6. Answer B.

- The auxiliary cluster (Cluster 2) is more C-terminal

- The auxiliary cluster is a separate [4Fe-4S] cluster with its own set of four cysteine ligands
